### Tinker: Diffusion's Gift to 3D--Multi-View Consistent Editing From Sparse Inputs without Per-Scene Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14811v1)
- **Authors**: Canyu Zhao, Xiaoman Li, Tianjian Feng, Zhiyue Zhao, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen
- **Abstract**: We introduce Tinker, a versatile framework for high-fidelity 3D editing that operates in both one-shot and few-shot regimes without any per-scene finetuning. Unlike prior techniques that demand extensive per-scene optimization to ensure multi-view consistency or to produce dozens of consistent edited input views, Tinker delivers robust, multi-view consistent edits from as few as one or two images. This capability stems from repurposing pretrained diffusion models, which unlocks their latent 3D awareness. To drive research in this space, we curate the first large-scale multi-view editing dataset and data pipeline, spanning diverse scenes and styles. Building on this dataset, we develop our framework capable of generating multi-view consistent edited views without per-scene training, which consists of two novel components: (1) Referring multi-view editor: Enables precise, reference-driven edits that remain coherent across all viewpoints. (2) Any-view-to-video synthesizer: Leverages spatial-temporal priors from video diffusion to perform high-quality scene completion and novel-view generation even from sparse inputs. Through extensive experiments, Tinker significantly reduces the barrier to generalizable 3D content creation, achieving state-of-the-art performance on editing, novel-view synthesis, and rendering enhancement tasks. We believe that Tinker represents a key step towards truly scalable, zero-shot 3D editing. Project webpage: https://aim-uofa.github.io/Tinker
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TINKER: Diffusion's Gift to 3D—Multi-View Consistent Editing From Sparse Inputs Without Per-Scene Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TINKER, a novel framework for 3D editing that aims to produce high-fidelity, multi-view consistent edits from sparse input views (one or a few images) *without* requiring per-scene optimization.  The core idea is to leverage pre-trained diffusion models, specifically their latent 3D awareness. TINKER comprises two key components: (1) a referring multi-view editor, which enables precise, reference-driven edits that remain coherent across different viewpoints, and (2) an any-view-to-video synthesizer, which leverages spatial-temporal priors from video diffusion models for scene completion and novel view generation, even from sparse inputs.  To facilitate research, the authors also contribute a new large-scale multi-view editing dataset and data pipeline. The paper demonstrates that TINKER significantly reduces the barrier to generalizable 3D content creation and achieves state-of-the-art performance in editing, novel-view synthesis, and rendering enhancement tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty in several aspects:
    *   **Zero/Few-Shot 3D Editing:** The primary novelty lies in achieving high-quality 3D editing without per-scene optimization.  This is a significant departure from many existing methods that require extensive fine-tuning, which is time-consuming and limits scalability.
    *   **Repurposing Pretrained Diffusion Models:**  The paper cleverly exploits the latent 3D awareness of pre-trained diffusion models. This avoids training 3D-aware models from scratch, reducing data and computational requirements. The specific technique of concatenating images and fine-tuning the model to understand reference editing is also a novel contribution.
    *   **Sparse Input Editing:** The ability to perform high-quality edits from only one or two input images is a significant advancement.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** Creating and releasing a large-scale multi-view consistent editing dataset addresses a critical gap in the field.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Scalability:** The elimination of per-scene optimization is a major step toward truly scalable 3D editing.  This could democratize 3D content creation, making it accessible to a wider range of users.
    *   **Generalizability:** The framework's ability to generalize to diverse scenes and styles is another crucial aspect.
    *   **Impact on Future Research:**  The dataset and the novel framework itself provide a strong foundation for future research in generalizable, user-friendly 3D content creation.
    *   **Integration of 2D/3D:** The seamless integration of recent advancements in 2D diffusion models into the 3D domain is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear Problem Definition: The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing 3D editing techniques.
    *   Elegant Solution: The proposed framework is well-designed and leverages the power of pre-trained diffusion models effectively.
    *   Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper includes extensive experiments and comparisons with state-of-the-art methods. The quantitative metrics are well-chosen. The qualitative results are compelling.
    *   Significant Contribution: The dataset is a valuable resource for the research community.
    *   Well Written: The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dependency on Depth Estimation: The scene completion module relies on depth estimation, and inaccuracies in depth could propagate to the final results.
    *   Limited Geometric Deformations: The current approach is limited in its ability to handle edits involving large geometric deformations.
    *   Dataset Bias: While the synthesized dataset is valuable, it inherits any biases present in the foundation models used to generate it.
    *   Potential for Artifacts: Diffusion models are known to sometimes generate artifacts or inconsistencies, and these could appear in the final edited 3D scenes. The paper doesn't explicitly address this issue.

* **Room for improvement:**

* The manuscript doesn't show failure cases which are necessary to understand its limitation
* Further exploration of user-driven control could enhance its applicability.
* Expand on the discussion of long-term potential, considering advancements in both diffusion models and 3D representation.

**Overall:**

TINKER represents a significant advancement in 3D editing. The zero/few-shot editing capability, the clever use of pre-trained diffusion models, and the contribution of a new dataset are all valuable contributions. While the method has limitations, the overall impact is substantial.

**Score: 8.5**

**Justification:** The paper provides a well-designed framework and a valuable new dataset that addresses significant challenges in 3D editing, exhibiting a strong novelty. It enables zero/few-shot 3D editing with high fidelity and consistency without requiring per-scene optimization. It is also well written, with comprehensive experiments and compelling qualitative results. The significant impact of the project lowers the usage of 3DGS editing a lot. The limitations are acceptable and expected at this stage, given the trade-offs involved.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### TransLight: Image-Guided Customized Lighting Control with Generative Decoupling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14814v1)
- **Authors**: Zongming Li, Lianghui Zhu, Haocheng Shen, Longjin Ran, Wenyu Liu, Xinggang Wang
- **Abstract**: Most existing illumination-editing approaches fail to simultaneously provide customized control of light effects and preserve content integrity. This makes them less effective for practical lighting stylization requirements, especially in the challenging task of transferring complex light effects from a reference image to a user-specified target image. To address this problem, we propose TransLight, a novel framework that enables high-fidelity and high-freedom transfer of light effects. Extracting the light effect from the reference image is the most critical and challenging step in our method. The difficulty lies in the complex geometric structure features embedded in light effects that are highly coupled with content in real-world scenarios. To achieve this, we first present Generative Decoupling, where two fine-tuned diffusion models are used to accurately separate image content and light effects, generating a newly curated, million-scale dataset of image-content-light triplets. Then, we employ IC-Light as the generative model and train our model with our triplets, injecting the reference lighting image as an additional conditioning signal. The resulting TransLight model enables customized and natural transfer of diverse light effects. Notably, by thoroughly disentangling light effects from reference images, our generative decoupling strategy endows TransLight with highly flexible illumination control. Experimental results establish TransLight as the first method to successfully transfer light effects across disparate images, delivering more customized illumination control than existing techniques and charting new directions for research in illumination harmonization and editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the TransLight paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TransLight, a novel image-guided method for transferring light effects from a reference image to a target image. The core idea is to decouple light effects from content using a generative decoupling strategy based on fine-tuned diffusion models. This process involves creating a large dataset of image-content-light triplets and then training a model to transfer the extracted light effects while allowing flexible control over their position, direction, and intensity. The authors claim TransLight is the first method to achieve high-fidelity and high-freedom light effect transfer, outperforming existing illumination editing techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the generative decoupling approach for isolating light effects. While style transfer and illumination editing are established fields, the specific task of transferring complex light effects *and* offering customized control over these effects is a less explored niche. Building an image-content-light triplet dataset is also a significant engineering contribution to enable the training.  However, the components themselves – fine-tuning diffusion models and using ControlNet – are not groundbreaking in isolation. A good portion of the paper revolves around data engineering (creating a dataset), which is important but doesn't always equate to theoretical advancement.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the practical applicability of the method.  If TransLight genuinely enables users to easily transfer and manipulate light effects in a customized way, it could have a solid impact on image editing workflows. The results demonstrate improved fidelity of transferred light effects and more customized illumination control than existing techniques.  This can open the door to applications in creative image editing, portrait enhancement, and realistic scene composition. The quantitative evaluations also show better performance compared to existing methods in generating similar lighting effects from content. However, the limited quantitative comparisons (especially the absence of comparisons with other style transfer or light removal methods) weaken the significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a practical and relatively unexplored problem.
    *   The Generative Decoupling approach shows promise for isolating and transferring light effects.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate visually compelling light effect transfers and adjustments.
    *   Construction of a large-scale image-content-light dataset.
    * The Light FID metric is insightful for evaluating the quality of the light transfer.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The technical approach is based on existing techniques (diffusion models, ControlNet). The core innovation is in adapting and combining these methods.
    *   Limited quantitative comparisons, especially with more recent style transfer methods or dedicated light removal/manipulation techniques. The method is compared to the ground truth image, however, this does not show the ability of *transferring* lighting between images. It seems as if it only extracts lighting effects.
    *   The reliance on a massive dataset may limit the generalizability of the method to new scenarios or types of light effects.
    * The results shown are carefully curated and highlight the successes of the method. The paper doesn't adequately address limitations and potential failure cases of the algorithm.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The approach has the potential to influence research in illumination harmonization, style transfer, and image editing. Future works may expand upon this idea to achieve better light manipulation or explore other applications.

**Justification for Score:**

TransLight presents a useful contribution to the field of image editing, particularly in the area of controlled light effect transfer. The novelty stems from the combination of generative decoupling and a data-driven approach to address this specific task. While the individual techniques are not entirely new, the overall system and the results are interesting. The potential for practical applications in creative image editing is evident.

However, the limitations in the quantitative evaluation, the lack of comparison with a broader range of competing methods, and the reliance on existing techniques bring the score down. The paper is well executed, but lacks a truly disruptive innovation.  It is more of a solid engineering contribution and system integration than a theoretical breakthrough.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation vs. Long-Context Input for Clinical Reasoning over EHRs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14817v1)
- **Authors**: Skatje Myers, Dmitriy Dligach, Timothy A. Miller, Samantha Barr, Yanjun Gao, Matthew Churpek, Anoop Mayampurath, Majid Afshar
- **Abstract**: Electronic health records (EHRs) are long, noisy, and often redundant, posing a major challenge for the clinicians who must navigate them. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising solution for extracting and reasoning over this unstructured text, but the length of clinical notes often exceeds even state-of-the-art models' extended context windows. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) offers an alternative by retrieving task-relevant passages from across the entire EHR, potentially reducing the amount of required input tokens. In this work, we propose three clinical tasks designed to be replicable across health systems with minimal effort: 1) extracting imaging procedures, 2) generating timelines of antibiotic use, and 3) identifying key diagnoses. Using EHRs from actual hospitalized patients, we test three state-of-the-art LLMs with varying amounts of provided context, using either targeted text retrieval or the most recent clinical notes. We find that RAG closely matches or exceeds the performance of using recent notes, and approaches the performance of using the models' full context while requiring drastically fewer input tokens. Our results suggest that RAG remains a competitive and efficient approach even as newer models become capable of handling increasingly longer amounts of text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper evaluates the effectiveness of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) compared to long-context input for clinical reasoning tasks using Electronic Health Records (EHRs).  It investigates three clinical tasks: imaging procedure extraction, antibiotic timeline generation, and diagnosis generation. The study compares the performance of three state-of-the-art Large Language Models (LLMs) given varying amounts of context, either through RAG or by providing recent clinical notes. The key finding is that RAG achieves comparable or even superior performance to using recent notes and closely matches the performance of using much larger contexts, while requiring significantly fewer input tokens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its empirical evaluation of RAG versus long-context models on *real-world* clinical data and *three well-defined and important tasks*. While RAG is not a new concept, its application in clinical settings, specifically comparing it against the paradigm shift toward long-context models, represents a useful contribution. Prior work is adequately cited, placing the current work within the context of existing literature. However, the core ideas build upon existing research and do not break new ground. The definition of replicable and realistic tasks adds some practical value.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper is moderate. Showing that RAG remains a competitive and token-efficient approach in a real-world clinical scenario has practical implications. EHRs are inherently long and complex, and computational efficiency is a crucial consideration. The paper highlights the potential for RAG to reduce computational costs, improve accuracy through noise reduction, and mitigate the "lost-in-the-middle" effect. The study also identifies task-specific performance differences and the importance of targeted retrieval strategies. However, the limited generalizability due to data limitations and specific model choices diminishes the impact. The defined tasks address realistic clinical needs but are not necessarily transformative. The study could potentially influence clinical practice by demonstrating the utility of RAG for information extraction from EHRs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The use of real-world EHR data and the definition of replicable clinical tasks are strong points.
    *   The comparison of RAG with recent-notes and long-context baselines is a valuable contribution.
    *   The paper quantifies token efficiency alongside performance metrics, providing a practical perspective.
    *   The tasks are grounded in actual clinical workflows.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The data is from a single institution, potentially limiting generalizability.
    *   The study is focused on a relatively small set of models and tasks.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper error analysis to understand the types of errors RAG and long-context models make.
    *   The prompts are not thoroughly discussed. The simple nature of the retrieval queries might be a limitation, and experimenting with more complex queries could be beneficial. The authors mention the lack of notes authored by ID physicians; the availability of these types of notes could have further enhanced the model's performance.
    *   The gains of long-context models over recent notes and RAG were often small.

*   **Potential Influence:** The findings can inform clinical researchers and practitioners about the utility of RAG in EHR analysis and provide guidance for the development of clinically relevant AI tools.  It reinforces the potential of RAG as a cost-effective approach even with expanding context windows.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**
The paper presents a valuable empirical evaluation comparing RAG and long-context models for clinical reasoning, but the novelty of the approach itself is somewhat limited. The use of real-world data and replicable tasks is a strength. However, the single-institution data, smaller improvements with longer context and a more limited number of models and tasks, restricts its overall impact. Therefore, the score reflects the practical relevance of the study but also acknowledges its limitations.

### Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Across Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14828v1)
- **Authors**: Josh Barua, Seun Eisape, Kayo Yin, Alane Suhr
- **Abstract**: Scaling inference through long chains-of-thought (CoTs) has unlocked impressive reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs), yet the reasoning process remains almost exclusively English-centric. We construct translated versions of two popular English reasoning datasets, fine-tune Qwen 2.5 (7B) and Qwen 3 (8B) models, and present a systematic study of long CoT generation across French, Japanese, Latvian, and Swahili. Our experiments reveal three key findings. First, the efficacy of using English as a pivot language varies by language: it provides no benefit for French, improves performance when used as the reasoning language for Japanese and Latvian, and proves insufficient for Swahili where both task comprehension and reasoning remain poor. Second, extensive multilingual pretraining in Qwen 3 narrows but does not eliminate the cross-lingual performance gap. A lightweight fine-tune using only 1k traces still improves performance by over 30\% in Swahili. Third, data quality versus scale trade-offs are language dependent: small, carefully curated datasets suffice for English and French, whereas larger but noisier corpora prove more effective for Swahili and Latvian. Together, these results clarify when and why long CoTs transfer across languages and provide translated datasets to foster equitable multilingual reasoning research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning across multiple languages, addressing the English-centric focus of much current research. The authors create translated versions of two popular English reasoning datasets (s1k and Bespoke-Stratos-17k) into French, Japanese, Latvian, and Swahili. They then fine-tune Qwen 2.5 (7B) and Qwen 3 (8B) models and conduct experiments to assess the effects of pivot-language reasoning (using English), the impact of extensive multilingual pretraining, and the trade-offs between data quality and quantity in fine-tuning across these languages. The key findings are: 1) English as a pivot language is not a universal solution and its effectiveness varies significantly by language resource availability. 2) Extensive multilingual pretraining reduces performance gaps, and even a small fine-tune improves results, particularly for low-resource languages like Swahili. 3)  The balance between data quality and quantity in fine-tuning data is language dependent.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses an Important Gap:** The paper tackles a critical issue in the field of LLMs: the lack of equitable multilingual reasoning capabilities.  It moves beyond simply using English as a universal pivot language and investigates nuances within different resource settings.
    * **Systematic Analysis:**  The study is well-designed, with carefully controlled experiments that systematically isolate the effects of different variables (pivot language, pretraining, data quality/quantity).
    * **Valuable Datasets:** The creation and release of translated datasets are a significant contribution, as they provide resources for future research in multilingual reasoning.
    * **Clear Findings:** The paper presents clear and actionable findings regarding the conditions under which different techniques are effective for various languages.  The results challenge the assumption that English pivoting is universally beneficial.
    * **Detailed Experiments:** Includes extensive details regarding hyperparameters and evaluation approaches, making the results highly reproducible.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Model Size:** While the choice of 7B/8B models provides a balance between computational constraints and reasoning ability, larger models might reveal different patterns or amplify the observed trends.  The models used, though strong, are not state-of-the-art and the conclusions may not perfectly translate to much larger models.
    * **Translation Quality:** While the paper uses a strong translation model (Gemini 2.0 Flash), automated translation can still introduce artifacts or inaccuracies.  The evaluation of translation quality is limited to BLEU/chrF++ scores, which may not fully capture the nuances of reasoning-relevant translations.
    * **Limited number of base models:** Only evaluated on Qwen 2.5 and 3, which is a limited number of base models.
    * **Dataset choices**: The paper is limited to only two reasoning datasets (s1k and Bespoke-Stratos-17k).
    * **Limited number of Low Resource Languages:** Only Swahili is explored as a low-resource language, which weakens the generality of the findings.
* **Novelty and Significance:**
    * The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic investigation of long CoT reasoning across a typologically diverse set of languages, considering the interplay between pivot language, pretraining, and data characteristics.  Previous work has often focused on shorter CoTs or primarily examined English as the pivot language.
    * The findings have significant implications for the development of more equitable and accessible LLMs. By demonstrating the limitations of English pivoting and the importance of language-specific fine-tuning, the paper encourages a more nuanced and targeted approach to multilingual reasoning.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by highlighting the complexities of multilingual reasoning and demonstrating that a one-size-fits-all approach (English pivoting) is insufficient. The creation of multilingual datasets is also a plus. While the paper could benefit from evaluation on more base models, larger models, more reasoning datasets, and more low-resource languages, the findings are clearly presented and the systematic approach makes it a valuable addition to the literature. The actionable insights and datasets will likely influence future research directions.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Universal and Transferable Adversarial Attack on Large Language Models Using Exponentiated Gradient Descent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14853v1)
- **Authors**: Sajib Biswas, Mao Nishino, Samuel Jacob Chacko, Xiuwen Liu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in critical applications, ensuring their robustness and safety alignment remains a major challenge. Despite the overall success of alignment techniques such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) on typical prompts, LLMs remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks enabled by crafted adversarial triggers appended to user prompts. Most existing jailbreak methods either rely on inefficient searches over discrete token spaces or direct optimization of continuous embeddings. While continuous embeddings can be given directly to selected open-source models as input, doing so is not feasible for proprietary models. On the other hand, projecting these embeddings back into valid discrete tokens introduces additional complexity and often reduces attack effectiveness. We propose an intrinsic optimization method which directly optimizes relaxed one-hot encodings of the adversarial suffix tokens using exponentiated gradient descent coupled with Bregman projection, ensuring that the optimized one-hot encoding of each token always remains within the probability simplex. We provide theoretical proof of convergence for our proposed method and implement an efficient algorithm that effectively jailbreaks several widely used LLMs. Our method achieves higher success rates and faster convergence compared to three state-of-the-art baselines, evaluated on five open-source LLMs and four adversarial behavior datasets curated for evaluating jailbreak methods. In addition to individual prompt attacks, we also generate universal adversarial suffixes effective across multiple prompts and demonstrate transferability of optimized suffixes to different LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces a novel adversarial attack method called Exponentiated Gradient Descent (EGD) for jailbreaking Large Language Models (LLMs).  Unlike many existing methods that either rely on inefficient discrete token space searches or direct optimization of continuous embeddings (which isn't always feasible for proprietary models), this approach directly optimizes relaxed one-hot encodings of adversarial suffix tokens. The key idea is to use exponentiated gradient descent coupled with Bregman projection, ensuring the optimized token encodings remain within the probability simplex.  The paper presents theoretical convergence guarantees for the method and demonstrates its effectiveness through experiments on several open-source LLMs.  It outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in success rate and convergence speed. The paper also demonstrates the generation of universal adversarial suffixes that are effective across multiple prompts and transferable to different LLMs, including proprietary models like GPT-3.5.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its use of exponentiated gradient descent for optimizing relaxed one-hot encodings of adversarial suffixes, intrinsically enforcing simplex constraints. This approach is arguably more elegant and potentially efficient than projection-based methods or discrete search techniques. The focus on universal and transferable attacks, while not entirely new *in principle*, is valuable and timely given the increasing deployment and reliance on LLMs. The convergence guarantees add a theoretical contribution, although the practical implications need careful consideration given the Remark 1 in the paper concerning non-smooth models.

*   **Significance:** The problem of jailbreaking LLMs is a critical one for the safe and ethical deployment of these models. The paper's proposed method addresses this problem with a theoretically sound and empirically validated approach. The ability to generate transferable and universal adversarial suffixes is particularly significant, as it highlights the vulnerabilities of even "aligned" LLMs and underscores the need for more robust defense mechanisms. The comparative evaluation against several strong baselines further bolsters the significance of the work. However, the limitation to white-box scenarios is a significant constraint that prevents it from being a general solution applicable to all models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear methodology.
    *   Theoretical convergence guarantees (though limited in scope).
    *   Empirical validation on multiple datasets and models.
    *   Comparison against relevant baselines.
    *   Demonstration of transferability and universality.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The convergence theorem relies on differentiability and a Lipschitz gradient, which might not hold for all LLM architectures (e.g., those with ReLU activations).
    *   The method is primarily evaluated on open-source models, limiting its direct applicability and validation on more powerful, closed-source models, such as GPT-4.
    *   The vulnerability of black-box models to these kinds of attacks wasn't tested, diminishing the paper's real-world applicability.
    *   While transferability to GPT-3.5 is shown, the lack of transferability to GPT-4o mini and Claude is a significant concern, pointing to the need for further investigation of the robustness of different alignment methods.
    *   The reliance on full model access (white-box setting) limits the applicability to real-world scenarios where attackers often have limited access to target models.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence the development of more robust defense mechanisms against adversarial attacks on LLMs. The EGD-based optimization technique may also inspire further research into alternative optimization methods for jailbreaking and related problems. Further work is needed to improve the generalisability and adaptability of the method towards more varied and complex LLMs. The work contributes to the broader understanding of LLM vulnerabilities, highlighting the importance of robust alignment techniques and the need for ongoing research in this area.

**Score: 7.0**

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a novel and effective method for jailbreaking LLMs, supported by theoretical analysis and empirical evidence. It addresses a crucial problem with practical implications. However, the convergence limitations, the primary focus on open-source models, the restriction to white-box settings, and the limited transferability to more robustly aligned models significantly restrict the paper's impact and overall score. It is a solid contribution, but more extensive validation and addressing the weaknesses are necessary for higher recognition.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Prompting Brain: Neurocognitive Markers of Expertise in Guiding Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14869v1)
- **Authors**: Hend Al-Khalifa, Raneem Almansour, Layan Abdulrahman Alhuasini, Alanood Alsaleh, Mohamad-Hani Temsah, Mohamad-Hani_Temsah, Ashwag Rafea S Alruwaili
- **Abstract**: Prompt engineering has rapidly emerged as a critical skill for effective interaction with large language models (LLMs). However, the cognitive and neural underpinnings of this expertise remain largely unexplored. This paper presents findings from a cross-sectional pilot fMRI study investigating differences in brain functional connectivity and network activity between experts and intermediate prompt engineers. Our results reveal distinct neural signatures associated with higher prompt engineering literacy, including increased functional connectivity in brain regions such as the left middle temporal gyrus and the left frontal pole, as well as altered power-frequency dynamics in key cognitive networks. These findings offer initial insights into the neurobiological basis of prompt engineering proficiency. We discuss the implications of these neurocognitive markers in Natural Language Processing (NLP). Understanding the neural basis of human expertise in interacting with LLMs can inform the design of more intuitive human-AI interfaces, contribute to cognitive models of LLM interaction, and potentially guide the development of AI systems that better align with human cognitive workflows. This interdisciplinary approach aims to bridge the gap between human cognition and machine intelligence, fostering a deeper understanding of how humans learn and adapt to complex AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a pilot fMRI study investigating the neurocognitive basis of expertise in prompt engineering for large language models (LLMs). The researchers compared the brain activity of expert and intermediate prompt engineers, finding differences in functional connectivity and network power ratios. Experts exhibited increased functional connectivity in brain regions associated with language processing (left middle temporal gyrus) and higher-order cognition (left frontal pole), alongside altered power-frequency dynamics in key cognitive networks like the visual network, default mode network, and left lateral parietal network. The findings suggest that expert prompt engineering involves more efficient and stable neural processing, potentially reflecting a refined ability to access language representations, plan strategically, and manage cognitive demands. The paper concludes by discussing implications for NLP and HCI design, advocating for interfaces that support semantic retrieval, visual reasoning, and executive planning to facilitate easier transitions from novice to expert.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its pioneering nature. It's one of the first studies to directly investigate the neural correlates of prompt engineering expertise. While other research has explored AI and cognitive biases in language models, and more broadly, expertise in related cognitive skills like programming, this study specifically targets the cognitive processes underpinning effective human interaction with LLMs via prompt engineering. This is a relatively untouched area, making the work novel.

**Significance:** The findings, while preliminary, have potential significance for several fields.

*   **NLP:** Understanding the neural basis of prompt engineering can inform the development of more intuitive and human-aligned AI systems. By identifying the cognitive processes experts engage in, designers can create LLMs that better align with human cognitive workflows.
*   **HCI:** The research suggests specific design principles for interfaces that support semantic retrieval, visual reasoning, and executive planning. This could lead to more effective training tools and adaptive scaffolds for prompt engineering.
*   **Cognitive Neuroscience:** The study contributes to our understanding of how the brain adapts to complex human-computer interactions and provides a basis for further research in this rapidly evolving field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Pilot Study Limitations:** The paper clearly acknowledges several limitations inherent to its pilot nature. The small sample size (N=22) limits the generalizability of the findings. A larger sample would be beneficial to increase statistical power and account for individual variability.
*   **Cross-Sectional Design:** The cross-sectional design cannot establish causality. It's impossible to determine whether the observed neural differences are a cause or consequence of prompt engineering expertise. Longitudinal studies are needed to address this.
*   **PELS Cutoff Score:** The cutoff score used to classify participants as "expert" or "intermediate" is somewhat arbitrary. While justified by expert review and pilot data, more rigorous psychometric validation is required to establish a statistically optimized threshold.
*   **Resting-State fMRI:** The reliance on resting-state fMRI provides valuable insights into intrinsic network activity, but it is an indirect measurement of the specific cognitive processes. The study would benefit from including task-based fMRI paradigms to directly assess neural activity during prompt formulation and refinement. The authors acknowledge this.

**Strengths:**

* Interdisciplinary Approach: Combining cognitive neuroscience with NLP and HCI is a strength, broadening the potential impact.
* Well-Defined Methodology: The description of the fMRI data analysis is clear and well-justified, including preprocessing, ICA, and seed-to-voxel connectivity analyses.
* Clear Acknowledgment of Limitations: The authors are transparent about the limitations of the study, which enhances its credibility and sets clear directions for future research.
* Discussion of Alternative Explanations: The paper addresses alternative interpretations of the fALFF results, showing awareness of potential confounding factors.

**Potential Influence:**

The study has the potential to spark further research at the intersection of cognitive neuroscience, NLP, and HCI. It lays the groundwork for developing cognitively informed interfaces, training programs, and AI systems that are better aligned with human cognitive processes.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the limitations of a pilot study, particularly the small sample size and cross-sectional design, and the reliance on resting-state fMRI, a higher score is difficult to justify. However, the study's novelty, potential significance, and clear methodology warrant a score above the median. This is a starting point.
This first investigation is novel and has the ability to inform future design, however, it needs to be reinforced by follow up investigation.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: q-bio.NC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Squeezed Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14871v1)
- **Authors**: Jyotirmai Singh, Samar Khanna, James Burgess
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models typically inject isotropic Gaussian noise, disregarding structure in the data. Motivated by the way quantum squeezed states redistribute uncertainty according to the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, we introduce Squeezed Diffusion Models (SDM), which scale noise anisotropically along the principal component of the training distribution. As squeezing enhances the signal-to-noise ratio in physics, we hypothesize that scaling noise in a data-dependent manner can better assist diffusion models in learning important data features. We study two configurations: (i) a Heisenberg diffusion model that compensates the scaling on the principal axis with inverse scaling on orthogonal directions and (ii) a standard SDM variant that scales only the principal axis. Counterintuitively, on CIFAR-10/100 and CelebA-64, mild antisqueezing - i.e. increasing variance on the principal axis - consistently improves FID by up to 15% and shifts the precision-recall frontier toward higher recall. Our results demonstrate that simple, data-aware noise shaping can deliver robust generative gains without architectural changes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Squeezed Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Squeezed Diffusion Models (SDM)," a modification to standard diffusion models where noise is added anisotropically, specifically along the principal component of the data distribution.  Inspired by quantum squeezing, where uncertainty is redistributed to enhance signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), the authors hypothesize that data-dependent noise scaling can improve the learning of important data features. They investigate two variants: (1) a Heisenberg diffusion model (HDM) that scales the principal direction and inversely scales orthogonal directions, and (2) a "standard" SDM that only scales the principal direction.  Counterintuitively, the results on CIFAR-10/100 and CelebA-64 show that mild *anti*-squeezing (increasing variance along the principal axis) consistently improves FID scores, suggesting an improvement in generative quality despite seemingly degrading SNR. The paper also demonstrates improved recall in precision-recall analysis with antisqueezing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in the adaptation of the concept of quantum squeezing to the domain of diffusion models. While data-dependent noise scheduling has been explored before [5], using a PCA-aligned, single-parameter scaling inspired by quantum metrology is a fresh approach.  The counterintuitive finding that *anti*-squeezing can improve performance also adds to the novelty.

**Significance:**

The potential significance stems from the fact that it provides a simple yet effective way to improve the performance of diffusion models without requiring architectural changes.  The improvements in FID scores, especially the ~15% on CIFAR-10, are noteworthy.  The demonstration of improvements in recall, by sacrificing a bit of precision, is also a relevant contribution to understanding how generative models capture the true data distribution. However, the results are limited to relatively small datasets (CIFAR, CelebA-64). Testing on larger, more complex datasets like ImageNet would significantly strengthen the significance. The improvement isn't huge and relies heavily on hyperparameter tuning for `s`.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Motivation:** The connection to quantum squeezing and the Heisenberg uncertainty principle provides a compelling theoretical foundation.
*   **Simplicity:**  The method is relatively easy to implement and integrates seamlessly with existing diffusion model frameworks.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides experimental results on multiple datasets and reports FID scores, precision-recall analysis and has ablations, lending credence to the claims.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are restricted to relatively low-resolution image datasets.  Generalizability to larger, more complex datasets needs to be demonstrated.
*   **Lack of Deep Theoretical Understanding:** While the quantum squeezing analogy is appealing, the paper doesn't fully explain *why* antisqueezing works. The intuition behind the improvement from antisqueezing is not adequately explored.
*   **Sensitivity to Hyperparameters:** The performance is dependent on the squeezing strength parameter `s`, requiring careful tuning. More analysis of the sensitivity to `s` and potentially adaptive methods for setting the value would be helpful.
*   **Marginal Improvement:** The improvement in FID score is notable but not groundbreaking. It is not clear whether it would translate into a meaningful advantage in downstream applications.
*   **Limited Novelty beyond PCA:** A PCA alignment on pixel space is a very specific application and the link to the underlying image statistics is pretty weak. It is not clear how the method would extend to other kinds of modalities where this alignment would not hold.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence future research in diffusion models by encouraging the exploration of data-dependent noise schedules and by highlighting the potential benefits of manipulating noise in ways that might seem counterintuitive. It might also inspire researchers to draw inspiration from other fields, such as quantum metrology, for improving generative models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to noise scheduling in diffusion models with promising empirical results. However, its scope is limited by the small datasets used, the lack of a deep theoretical explanation of the observed phenomena, and the relatively incremental improvement in performance.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Quantization Meets dLLMs: A Systematic Study of Post-training Quantization for Diffusion LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14896v1)
- **Authors**: Haokun Lin, Haobo Xu, Yichen Wu, Ziyu Guo, Renrui Zhang, Zhichao Lu, Ying Wei, Qingfu Zhang, Zhenan Sun
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have introduced a promising alternative to autoregressive (AR) LLMs for natural language generation tasks, leveraging full attention and denoising-based decoding strategies. However, the deployment of these models on edge devices remains challenging due to their massive parameter scale and high resource demands. While post-training quantization (PTQ) has emerged as a widely adopted technique for compressing AR LLMs, its applicability to dLLMs remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present the first systematic study on quantizing diffusion-based language models. We begin by identifying the presence of activation outliers, characterized by abnormally large activation values that dominate the dynamic range. These outliers pose a key challenge to low-bit quantization, as they make it difficult to preserve precision for the majority of values. More importantly, we implement state-of-the-art PTQ methods and conduct a comprehensive evaluation across multiple task types and model variants. Our analysis is structured along four key dimensions: bit-width, quantization method, task category, and model type. Through this multi-perspective evaluation, we offer practical insights into the quantization behavior of dLLMs under different configurations. We hope our findings provide a foundation for future research in efficient dLLM deployment. All codes and experimental setups will be released to support the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic study of post-training quantization (PTQ) for diffusion large language models (dLLMs).  It addresses the current gap in research on efficiently deploying dLLMs, which have shown promise but are computationally demanding. The authors identify activation outliers in dLLMs, similar to those found in autoregressive LLMs, and investigate how various PTQ techniques perform on dLLMs under different configurations (bit-width, quantization method, task category, and model type). Key findings include: 4-bit quantization being suitable for weight-only PTQ, 8-bit for weight-activation PTQ, GPTQ outperforming AWQ for weight-only PTQ, rotation-based methods (DuQuant) surpassing SmoothQuant for weight-activation PTQ, and the observation that instruction-tuned models are more resilient to quantization than base models.  The paper releases code and experimental setups to support further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **First Systematic Study:**  The paper claims to be the *first* systematic study of PTQ for dLLMs. This is a significant strength if true, as it fills a clear void in the existing literature.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates multiple PTQ methods across different bit-widths, task categories, and model variants. This multi-faceted approach provides valuable insights into the behavior of quantized dLLMs. The breadth of experimentation is commendable.
    * **Practical Insights:** The findings offer actionable recommendations for quantizing dLLMs, such as the appropriate bit-widths for weight-only and weight-activation quantization and the choice of quantization methods.
    * **Reproducibility:** The release of code and experimental setups is crucial for reproducibility and facilitates future research in this area.
    * **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** The paper tackles a critical issue in dLLM deployment: high computational cost.
    * **Identification of Activation Outliers:** Successfully identifies and analyzes activation outliers in dLLMs. This analysis helps explain performance differences between different quantization techniques.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Incremental Novelty in Techniques:** While the application to dLLMs is novel, the PTQ techniques themselves (GPTQ, AWQ, SmoothQuant, DuQuant) are not new. The novelty primarily lies in their application and evaluation within the dLLM context.
    * **Limited Innovation in Outlier Mitigation:**  The paper finds that existing outlier mitigation techniques are insufficient for low-bit quantization in dLLMs, but doesn't propose novel solutions.
    * **Task-Specific Degradation:** The paper observes significant performance drops on math and code generation tasks, suggesting that existing PTQ methods may not be well-suited for these tasks. Further research is required to address this issue.
    * **Lack of Real-World Deployment Results:** The paper focuses primarily on downstream task performance. Results demonstrating actual reductions in memory usage or inference time on specific hardware platforms would significantly strengthen the impact of the findings.
    * **Reliance on Existing Benchmarks:** While comprehensive, the evaluation relies primarily on standard benchmarks.  More tailored benchmarks or real-world applications would provide a more convincing evaluation.
    * **Limited Comparison to Other Compression Methods:** The paper only compares quantization techniques.  A comparison with other compression methods such as pruning and knowledge distillation would offer a more holistic view of dLLM compression.

* **Significance:**

    * This paper significantly advances the field by providing a thorough evaluation of existing quantization methods on a new and important class of models. By identifying the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches, the paper lays the groundwork for future research on efficient dLLM deployment.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable and thorough investigation of applying existing PTQ techniques to a new class of models (dLLMs), which are becoming increasingly important. The extensive experimental analysis, coupled with the release of code, will be beneficial to the research community. However, the incremental nature of applying existing methods without proposing significant innovations and the lack of real-world deployment results limit its overall impact.

### Improving LLMs for Machine Translation Using Synthetic Preference Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14951v1)
- **Authors**: Dario Vajda, Domen Vreš, Marko Robnik-Šikonja
- **Abstract**: Large language models have emerged as effective machine translation systems. In this paper, we explore how a general instruction-tuned large language model can be improved for machine translation using relatively few easily produced data resources. Using Slovene as a use case, we improve the GaMS-9B-Instruct model using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training on a programmatically curated and enhanced subset of a public dataset. As DPO requires pairs of quality-ranked instances, we generated its training dataset by translating English Wikipedia articles using two LLMs, GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct. We ranked the resulting translations based on heuristics coupled with automatic evaluation metrics such as COMET. The evaluation shows that our fine-tuned model outperforms both models involved in the dataset generation. In comparison to the baseline models, the fine-tuned model achieved a COMET score gain of around 0.04 and 0.02, respectively, on translating Wikipedia articles. It also more consistently avoids language and formatting errors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores how to improve a general instruction-tuned large language model (LLM) for machine translation (MT) using relatively few resources, specifically focusing on English-to-Slovene translation.  It improves a GaMS-9B-Instruct model by using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training on a programmatically curated subset of a public dataset. The preference data required by DPO is generated by translating English Wikipedia articles with two LLMs (GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct).  The resulting translations are then ranked using a combination of heuristics (identifying language errors and truncations) and automatic evaluation metrics like COMET.  The fine-tuned model outperforms both models used for dataset generation, achieving improved COMET scores and better consistency in avoiding language and formatting errors. The authors release their fine-tuned model and the code for their data generation and fine-tuning pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *methodology* for generating training data for preference learning in machine translation, particularly for a low-resource language like Slovene.  While using DPO is not new in itself, the authors demonstrate a practical and resource-efficient way to create a preference dataset *without* human annotation. This is significant.  The approach of using two LLMs to generate translations, combined with rule-based and metric-based filtering, is a clever way to bootstrap a training set where human annotation would be too costly or time-consuming. The synthetic generation and combination of rule-based and automatic-metric scoring to create preference data is the major contribution of this paper.

*   **Significance:** The significance is two-fold:

    1.  **Practical contribution:** The paper provides a recipe for improving LLM-based MT for low-resource languages, which can have a real impact on accessibility to information and communication for speakers of those languages. The release of the model and code enables others to build upon and extend this work.
    2.  **Methodological contribution:** The study demonstrates a valuable technique for generating preference data for LLMs when direct human feedback is unavailable or impractical. The methodological approach and results could have a positive impact for other tasks as well, such as summarization, generation of code, creative text generation, and question answering.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenge of building high-quality MT systems for low-resource languages where data is scarce.
    *   **Well-Described Methodology:** The data generation, filtering, and fine-tuning processes are explained in sufficient detail for reproducibility.  The authors acknowledge and attempt to mitigate the potential limitations of synthetic data.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:**  The paper includes both automatic metrics (COMET, BLEU) and an analysis of error types, providing a comprehensive assessment of the model's performance.  It also compared the generated translations to the base models on the basis of language error and truncation error rate.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of the model and code are important contributions to the community.
    *   **Quantifiable Results:** Improvement in COMET scores are presented along with quantifiable reductions in the error rate.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The study is focused on a single language pair (English-Slovene).  While the method is claimed to be language-agnostic, it would be stronger with experiments on another low-resource language. The generalizability of the findings needs further validation.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** The reliance on Wikipedia and CC-News might introduce biases into the model. The authors acknowledge this and propose incorporating other domains in future work, which is important. Also, the creation of the benchmark uses a similar method to the fine-tuning of the model.
    *   **Evaluation limitations:** The evaluation relies on automatic metrics like COMET and BLEU, which can be imperfect proxies for human judgment.  While the error analysis provides some insight, a human evaluation would further strengthen the results.
    *   **Limited comparisons:** Although the DPO fine-tuned model is an improvement, the performance against current state-of-the-art commercial systems would further strengthen the impact of the paper.

*   **Impact:** The paper provides a practical and cost-effective approach to improving MT for low-resource languages. The methodology of generating synthetic preference data has potential implications beyond MT and could influence future research in preference alignment for LLMs. The work serves as a strong starting point for others aiming to tackle similar challenges with limited resources.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to machine translation, particularly in the context of low-resource languages. The use of synthetic preference data coupled with DPO is a resourceful and impactful approach. While there are some limitations in scope and evaluation, the paper offers a valuable methodology and resources for the community.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The score reflects the paper's clear novelty in its data generation method, its practical significance for low-resource languages, and the reasonable evaluation. It also takes into account limitations such as the narrow language focus, potential dataset biases, and incomplete evaluation using automated metrics. The paper provides incremental advancements to the existing technology but is unlikely to revolutionise machine translation. While the results are solid, the methodological approach and results are more broadly applicable than solely machine translation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Aura-CAPTCHA: A Reinforcement Learning and GAN-Enhanced Multi-Modal CAPTCHA System
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14976v1)
- **Authors**: Joydeep Chandra, Prabal Manhas, Ramanjot Kaur, Rashi Sahay
- **Abstract**: Aura-CAPTCHA was developed as a multi-modal CAPTCHA system to address vulnerabilities in traditional methods that are increasingly bypassed by AI technologies, such as Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and adversarial image processing. The design integrated Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) for generating dynamic image challenges, Reinforcement Learning (RL) for adaptive difficulty tuning, and Large Language Models (LLMs) for creating text and audio prompts. Visual challenges included 3x3 grid selections with at least three correct images, while audio challenges combined randomized numbers and words into a single task. RL adjusted difficulty based on incorrect attempts, response time, and suspicious user behavior. Evaluations on real-world traffic demonstrated a 92% human success rate and a 10% bot bypass rate, significantly outperforming existing CAPTCHA systems. The system provided a robust and scalable approach for securing online applications while remaining accessible to users, addressing gaps highlighted in previous research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Aura-CAPTCHA paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Aura-CAPTCHA, a novel multi-modal CAPTCHA system that leverages Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) to create adaptive audio-visual challenges. The system uses GANs (StyleGAN and AudioGAN) to generate dynamic image and audio content. An RL agent adjusts the challenge difficulty in real-time based on user interaction data, and a user interaction analysis module (using heuristics and SVM) detects bot-like behavior.  The goal is to improve security while maintaining usability for human users, addressing vulnerabilities in traditional CAPTCHA methods.  Experiments show Aura-CAPTCHA outperforms existing methods with a lower bypass rate and a high human success rate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multi-modal approach:**  Combining audio and visual elements is a promising strategy to enhance CAPTCHA robustness, leveraging human's superior ability to process multi-sensory information compared to current AI models. This is a well-justified approach with strong theoretical backing.
    *   **Adaptive Difficulty:** Using RL to adjust difficulty based on user behavior is a significant step towards balancing security and usability.  This addresses a key criticism of traditional CAPTCHAs which often frustrate legitimate users.  The Q-learning implementation is well-described.
    *   **GAN-based content generation:** Employing GANs to create dynamic and unpredictable challenges makes it harder for bots to exploit pre-defined datasets or patterns. This counters common attack vectors used against conventional CAPTCHAs.
    *   **User Interaction Analysis:**  Combining heuristics with an SVM classifier enhances the accuracy of bot detection, improving on reliance solely on heuristic-based methods.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents experimental results demonstrating Aura-CAPTCHA's superiority over traditional methods across various metrics, including bypass rate, human success rate, and response time.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Individual Components:** While the *integration* of RL, GANs, and user interaction analysis is novel, the individual components themselves are established techniques.  The paper's contribution is primarily in *how* these components are combined, rather than in ground-breaking advances in any single area.
    *   **Implementation Details:**  The paper lacks some crucial implementation details. It mentions using StyleGAN and AudioGAN, but doesn't fully specify training data or GAN architectures. Similarly, the features used in the SVM classifier (beyond the mentioned `avg_time_interval`, etc.) need more thorough definition.
    *   **Dataset for Training/Testing:** The evaluation lacks details on the dataset used for training the SVM classifier and the nature/source of the "real-world traffic" used in the experiments.  Without this information, the generalizability of the results is questionable.
    *   **Adversarial Robustness:**  While the paper claims resilience against AI-based attacks, it doesn't explicitly test Aura-CAPTCHA against specifically designed adversarial attacks (e.g., targeted GAN attacks, or sophisticated ASR systems crafted to bypass AudioGAN countermeasures). The results only show a low bypass rate against unspecified bots.
    *   **Accessibility Concerns:** The paper acknowledges accessibility, but does not go into detail on how Aura-CAPTCHA addresses those accessibility issues besides using a combination of audio and visual modalities. Further investigation and design for accessibility is needed.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important problem: securing online systems against sophisticated bots.
    *   The integration of RL and GANs in CAPTCHA design is a promising direction that can inspire further research.
    *   The user-centric approach, prioritizing both security and usability, is a valuable contribution to the field.

**Overall Score & Justification:**

I assign a score of **6/10**.

While Aura-CAPTCHA offers a solid and practical approach to improving CAPTCHAs, its novelty is limited by the reliance on established techniques. The integration of RL, GANs, and user behavior analysis is well-motivated, and the empirical results are encouraging. However, the lack of specific implementation details, limited evaluation against targeted adversarial attacks, and lack of a through discussion of accessibility issues detract from the paper's overall impact. It builds on existing research in a thoughtful way, showing promise for the future, but falls short of representing a radical breakthrough. With more thorough evaluation and implementation details, along with adversarial testing, it could achieve a much higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 0/10

### Multilingual Datasets for Custom Input Extraction and Explanation Requests Parsing in Conversational XAI Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14982v1)
- **Authors**: Qianli Wang, Tatiana Anikina, Nils Feldhus, Simon Ostermann, Fedor Splitt, Jiaao Li, Yoana Tsoneva, Sebastian Möller, Vera Schmitt
- **Abstract**: Conversational explainable artificial intelligence (ConvXAI) systems based on large language models (LLMs) have garnered considerable attention for their ability to enhance user comprehension through dialogue-based explanations. Current ConvXAI systems often are based on intent recognition to accurately identify the user's desired intention and map it to an explainability method. While such methods offer great precision and reliability in discerning users' underlying intentions for English, a significant challenge in the scarcity of training data persists, which impedes multilingual generalization. Besides, the support for free-form custom inputs, which are user-defined data distinct from pre-configured dataset instances, remains largely limited. To bridge these gaps, we first introduce MultiCoXQL, a multilingual extension of the CoXQL dataset spanning five typologically diverse languages, including one low-resource language. Subsequently, we propose a new parsing approach aimed at enhancing multilingual parsing performance, and evaluate three LLMs on MultiCoXQL using various parsing strategies. Furthermore, we present Compass, a new multilingual dataset designed for custom input extraction in ConvXAI systems, encompassing 11 intents across the same five languages as MultiCoXQL. We conduct monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual evaluations on Compass, employing three LLMs of varying sizes alongside BERT-type models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, aimed at providing a rigorous assessment of its novelty and significance.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of current Conversational Explainable AI (ConvXAI) systems, specifically the lack of multilingual datasets for intent recognition and the limited ability of users to provide custom inputs.  The authors introduce two new datasets:

*   **MultiCoXQL:** A multilingual extension of the existing CoXQL dataset for intent recognition, translated into five typologically diverse languages.
*   **Compass:** A new multilingual dataset designed for custom input extraction in ConvXAI systems, covering 11 intents across the same five languages.

The paper also proposes a novel parsing approach called Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP) to improve multilingual parsing performance. The authors evaluate GMP and other parsing strategies on MultiCoXQL and assess several LLMs and BERT-type models for intent recognition and custom input extraction on the Compass dataset, conducting monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper tackles a significant challenge in ConvXAI: the lack of multilingual resources and the restriction of users to predefined data instances.
    *   **Dataset Contributions:** The creation of MultiCoXQL and Compass is a valuable contribution to the field. These datasets provide resources for researchers to develop and evaluate multilingual ConvXAI systems that support custom inputs. The inclusion of a low-resource language (Telugu) is particularly noteworthy.
    *   **GMP Approach:** The Guided Multi-Prompt Parsing (GMP) method is a well-motivated approach that combines the strengths of existing methods. The results demonstrate a notable improvement in multilingual parsing accuracy compared to existing methods, especially for non-English languages.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation, considering monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual scenarios using a variety of models and metrics.
    *   **Code and Dataset Availability:** The authors provide access to the datasets and code, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Machine Translation:** The creation of MultiCoXQL and Compass relies heavily on machine translation. While the authors perform human evaluation and correction, the quality of the translated data may still be a concern, especially for the low-resource language, Telugu, potentially introducing bias. The low-resource aspect also means the data will be more limited than the original English.
    *   **Complexity and Scope:** The operations highlighted as not implemented could be a major value-add. The paper only addresses the intent recognition and custom input extraction, leaving the actual implementation of the new interactive operations within ConvXAI systems for future work. This limits the immediate impact of the datasets.
    *   **Error Analysis Depth:** While the paper includes an error analysis, a more in-depth investigation of the specific error patterns and their causes would provide valuable insights for future research.
    *   **Benchmarking Choices:** Some benchmark choices seem weaker (e.g., older BERT variants) while the landscape of SOTA models moves rapidly.
    *   **Limited Novelty in Model Choices:** While the focus is on dataset creation and the GMP parsing method, the model choices (mBERT, LLama3-8B, etc.) are standard and don't offer significant novelty in themselves. The novelty lies primarily in the application and evaluation within the ConvXAI context.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper introduces new resources that address a critical gap in the field of ConvXAI. The datasets and GMP method contribute to the development of more inclusive and user-friendly ConvXAI systems. The move towards supporting custom inputs is essential to enable users to work with *their* data. The work is significant, especially for researchers working on multilingual NLP, ConvXAI, and intent recognition/parsing.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    The datasets can become widely used benchmarks for evaluating multilingual intent recognition and custom input extraction in ConvXAI.  The GMP method may inspire further research on improved parsing approaches for multilingual NLP.

**Justification:**

The strengths of the paper – addressing a recognized gap, dataset contributions, the proposed parsing method, and comprehensive evaluation – justify a score above the average. The reliance on machine translation and the relatively shallow deployment, plus benchmarking against older models, slightly detracts from the score.

Score: 7.5
